Product-of-Gaussian-mixture diffusion models for joint nonlinear MRI reconstruction
Authors: Laurenz Nagler, Martin Zach, Thomas Pock
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
Recently, diffusion models have attracted considerable attention for magnetic resonance image reconstruction due to their high sample quality. However, most existing methods rely on large networks with opaque time conditioning mechanisms and require offline coil sensitivity estimation. This results in limited interpretability of the reconstruction process and reduced flexibility in the acquisition setup. To address these limitations, we jointly reconstruct the image and the coil sensitivities by combining the parameter-efficient product-of-Gaussian-mixture diffusion model as an image prior with a classical smoothness prior on the coil sensitivities. The proposed method is fast and robust to both contrast and anatomical distribution shifts as well as changing k-space trajectories. Finally, we propose a more expressive parameterization of the image prior which improves results in denoising and magnetic resonance image reconstruction.

Introduction

Magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) is a non-invasive imaging modality that is widely used in clinical practice. It provides high spatial resolution, versatile contrast mechanisms, and radiation-free operation. However, it typically requires long scan times which limits patient throughput and makes it prone to motion artifacts.

In recent years, numerous techniques that aim to reduce MRI acquisition time while preserving the diagnostic quality of the reconstructions [1–6] have been developed. As an example, parallel imaging (PI) methods exploit complementary information from multiple receiver coils to reconstruct images from partially observed Fourier-domain (k-space) measurements [7]. PI methods can be divided into two main categories. Auto-calibration methods—such as generalized autocalibrating partial parallel acquisition (GRAPPA) [2]—estimate missing data from auto-calibration lines (ACLs) in the k-space center to directly combine coil measurements. In contrast, image domain methods—such as sensitivity encoding (SENSE) [1]—rely on spatially varying coil sensitivities to combine the data in image space. The SENSE formulation leads to a nonlinear inverse problem (described in detail in Sect. 3) due to the unknown coil sensitivities.

Coil sensitivity estimation is traditionally addressed by: (i) Offline estimation of the coil sensitivities using an additional low-resolution prescan [8], followed by smoothing via low-pass filtering, locally fitting low-order polynomial [1, 9] or splines [10]. (ii) Post-processing estimation from the acquired data, including wavelet-based approaches [11, 12] and region growing with subsequent polynomial fitting [13]. One of the most popular methods in this category is ESPIRiT [14], which formulates coil sensitivity estimation as an eigenvalue problem for an operator derived from the ACL region of the data. The eigenvectors associated with the second-largest eigenvalue of this operator are then the estimates of the coil sensitivities. (iii) Joint reconstruction of both the image and the coil sensitivities. The authors of [15] parameterize the coil sensitivities with low-order polynomials and simultaneously optimize the image and coefficients of the polynomials. The authors of [16] and [17] solve the nonlinear problem using an iteratively regularized Gauss–Newton method with an L2 penalty on the image. In [16], the coil sensitivities are parameterized with a small number of basis functions, whereas [17] augments the objective function with classical penalties that promote smoothness. These works were extended in [18, 19] to incorporate variational penalties such as total variation (TV), total generalized variation (TGV)[ [20] or L1-wavelet on the image. Finally, in [21] the authors propose an alternating reconstruction method that exploits low-rank structure of the coil sensitivities. The methods in (i) and (ii) require additional time, either for acquiring the prescan or for post-processing the acquired data thus reducing flexibility. Joint reconstruction approaches in (iii) offer greater flexibility, but they are often computationally demanding and typically rely on hand-crafted regularization terms. In contrast, our method retains the flexibility of a joint reconstruction framework while replacing the hand-crafted regularization of the image with a data-driven prior.

Recently, data-driven reconstruction methods have attracted considerable attention in the MRI community. In early approaches, researchers modeled the map from the measured data to the reconstructed image with a neural network that was trained on a large dataset of such measurement–image pairs. Examples include pre-processing approaches that predict missing k-space data [22], post-processing approaches that refine a naive reconstruction [23], and learned iterative schemes [5, 24, 25]. All of these methods have in common that they incorporate the forward model in their training. The forward model accounts for coil sensitivities, k-space trajectories, and noise. Consequently, the performance of such methods typically deteriorates if in the data acquisition changes, for instance, if a different k-space trajectory is used (we demonstrate this in Sects. 5.2 and 5.3). In more recent approaches, methods that explicitly separate likelihood and prior became popular. These are naturally more robust to such variations. In the context of MRI, this also removes the need for difficult-to-obtain fully sampled multi-coil training data, since the prior can be learned from readily available DICOM images. Methods that follow this separation often leverage learned generative models such as generative adversarial networks [26], diffusion models [27–29], or models defined by a Gibbs distribution (often called energy-based models) [6, 30].

Among these, diffusion models have emerged as particularly powerful for MRI reconstruction and are currently state of the art. Nonetheless, several limitations remain: U-Net–type networks [31] typically approximate the score1 of the target distribution directly but are not explicitly constrained to form a conservative vector field. Consequently, theoretical conditions required for gradient-based Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) methods to generate samples according to the model distribution are not satisfied [32]. Additionally, the time conditioning of these networks—often implemented via Fourier features or positional embeddings [33, 34]—has no explicit link to the underlying stochastic differential equation (SDE) that governs the diffusion process. Finally, models with millions of parameters often entail substantial inference times and often require heuristics to handle data of arbitrary size [28, 35].

To address the limitations of typical networks used for diffusion models, Zach et al. [36] recently proposed the product-of-Gaussian-mixture diffusion model (PoGMDM), a diffusion model with a fields-of-experts–type architecture [37]. The model is parameter efficient, its time conditioning mechanism is linked to the underlying SDE, and the derived score forms a conservative vector field. In this work, we utilize a PoGMDM in a joint-reconstructing framework, thus eliminating the need for offline coil sensitivity estimation. Furthermore, we explore various possible extensions to the original PoGMDM architecture and evaluate their performance in both denoising and PI reconstruction.

Related work

Most existing methods for PI reconstruction assume knowledge of the coil sensitivities [24, 27, 29, 38, 39], which are typically obtained via offline estimation using algorithms such as ESPIRiT [14]. However, this approach is sensitive to patient motion, requires a calibration region at the center of k-space, and involves an additional gridding step for non-Cartesian k-space trajectories. Alternatively, Chung et al. [28] avoid explicit coil sensitivity estimation by reconstructing individual coil images and combining them into the final reconstruction through a pixel-wise root sum of squares (RSS) reduction. While this bypasses the need for the estimation of the coil sensitivities, the pixel-wise RSS reduction of individual coil images is known to yield biased reconstructions even in the noise-free case [40]. In addition, the computational cost of their approach grows linearly with the number of coils. The approach proposed in [41] addresses the problem of unknown coil sensitivities by a joint reconstruction aided by an additional diffusion prior on the coil sensitivities. Like the approach by Chung et al. [28], the computational complexity of this approach scales linearly with the number of coils and adds the overhead of training and tuning a second diffusion model. Similarly, in [42], separate diffusion priors for the image and coil sensitivities are used as substitutes for proximal operators within a proximal alternating linearized minimization (PALM) framework [43]. While this enables joint updates of both components, it again necessitates training an additional diffusion prior. Furthermore, the authors employ generatively learned models in maximum a posteriori (MAP)–based inference, which is known to be suboptimal [44]. Murata et al. [45] tackle the same problem by combining a diffusion prior for the image with a simple hand-crafted prior on the coil sensitivities. However, their approach relies on an efficient computation of the singular value decomposition of the forward operator, which is computationally infeasible for undersampled PI.

Our method also performs a joint reconstruction of the image and coil sensitivities, but differs in its modeling and computational requirements. Instead of learning a separate diffusion model for the sensitivities [41, 42], we employ a simple smoothness prior and update the image and coil sensitivities in an alternating fashion. This avoids training an additional diffusion model and maintains scalability with respect to the number of coils. Moreover, unlike [45], our approach does not require the computation of a singular value decomposition of the forward operator.

For more general surveys of diffusion models in inverse problems, we refer the reader to [46, 47].

Contributions

This work constitutes an extension to our preliminary results that were published in the conference paper [48]. There, we combine a PoGMDM as a prior on the images and a simple smoothness prior on the coil sensitivities in a joint reconstruction framework.

In our preliminary work, we evaluated the proposed joint reconstruction framework on coronal proton density (CORPD)-weighted knee images and assessed its robustness to shifts in the contrast mechanism using coronal proton density fat suppressed (CORPDFS)-weighted knee images. In the present work, we substantially extend this study and make the following contributions:We broaden the evaluation protocol to explicitly account for anatomy and contrast mechanism shifts by considering T1- and T2-weighted brain scans.We explore various possible extensions to the PoGMDM architecture and conduct extensive numerical experiments to compare their performance in both denoising and PI reconstruction.The remainder of the paper is organized as follows. We provide background on inverse problems, their Bayesian resolution, diffusion models, and their application to inverse problems in Sect. 2. We introduce the proposed joint reconstruction algorithm and discuss alternative parameterizations of the PoGMDM used within the algorithm in Sect. 3. In Sect. 5, we present extensive numerical results and compare the different PoGMDM parameterizations on in-distribution data and out-of-distribution data in the form of anatomy and contrast mechanism shifts. Finally, we conclude the paper and discuss future research directions in Sect. 6.

We broaden the evaluation protocol to explicitly account for anatomy and contrast mechanism shifts by considering T1- and T2-weighted brain scans.

We broaden the evaluation protocol to explicitly account for anatomy and contrast mechanism shifts by considering T1- and T2-weighted brain scans.

We explore various possible extensions to the PoGMDM architecture and conduct extensive numerical experiments to compare their performance in both denoising and PI reconstruction.

Background

Inverse problems

The forward operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{A} $$\end{document}A may be linear or nonlinear depending on the physics of the acquisition. For example, in the SENSE model for PI —where the image and the coil sensitivities are unknown—\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{A} $$\end{document}A is nonlinear (see Sect. 3.1 for details). In the Bayesian resolution of this problem, the unknown signal is modeled as a random variable, denoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{X} $$\end{document}X, with associated distribution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ p_\textbf{X} $$\end{document}pX, referred to as the prior. Given a signal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{x} $$\end{document}x, the likelihood \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ p_{\textbf{Y}|\textbf{X}}(\,\cdot \,\mid \textbf{x}) $$\end{document}pY|X(·∣x) describes the probability of observing any measurement, given the signal is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{x} $$\end{document}x. The likelihood is fully specified by the model of the measurement process and the model of the noise. Throughout this manuscript, we assume that the noise is Gaussian [49], which implies that the likelihood is given by2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} p_{\textbf{Y}|\textbf{X}}(\textbf{y}\mid \textbf{x}) \propto \exp \left( -\frac{1}{2\sigma _\text {n}^2} \Vert \textbf{A}(\textbf{x}) - \textbf{y}\Vert ^2\right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}pY|X(y∣x)∝exp-12σn2‖A(x)-y‖2,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _\text {n}^2$$\end{document}σn2 is the variance of the noise. We can combine the likelihood and the prior via Bayes’ rule, which states that the object of interest, the posterior \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ p_{\textbf{X}|\textbf{Y}} $$\end{document}pX|Y, is given by3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} p_{\textbf{X}|\textbf{Y}}(\textbf{x}\mid \textbf{y}) \propto p_{\textbf{Y}|\textbf{X}}(\textbf{y}\mid \textbf{x})p_{\textbf{X}}(\textbf{x}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}pX|Y(x∣y)∝pY|X(y∣x)pX(x).The resolution of the inverse problem is then to analyze the posterior distribution for a fixed prior, for instance, through the computation of Bayes estimators such as the MAP estimator (the mode of the posterior distribution) or the minimum mean squared error (MMSE) estimator (the expectation of the posterior distribution) [50]. Furthermore, the Bayesian approach to inverse problems enables uncertainty quantification of reconstructed signals. More background on Bayesian inverse problems can be found in [51]. Diffusion models are a popular choice for data-driven priors due to their ability to capture complex, high-dimensional distributions, their high-visual-quality samples, and their empirically demonstrated generalization capabilities [52].

Diffusion models

In the previous section, we showed that the modeling burden in the Bayesian resolution of inverse problems lies with the prior \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ p_\textbf{X} $$\end{document}pX. Modeling that prior from a finite set of samples is extremely challenging in the high-dimensional problems that are encountered in imaging tasks. A fruitful approach is to smooth the empirical distribution defined by its samples by a convolution with a Gaussian. This makes the distribution more regular and thereby eases the estimation task [53, 54]. However, the choice of the variance of the Gaussian is not obvious: When it is too small, the distribution is mostly unchanged and remains irregular and challenging to estimate. When it is too large, important features may be lost. To overcome this, Song et al. [53] propose to smooth the density at a sequence of noise scales and to learn a model conditioned on those. Approximate sampling from their model is achieved by using the unadjusted Langevin algorithm while gradually annealing the noise. In a follow-up work [55], Song et al. generalize this idea to SDEs by letting the number of noise scales go to infinity. A general form of the recovered diffusion process is given by4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{d}\textbf{X}_t = \textbf{f}(\textbf{X}_t, t)\textrm{d}t + g(t)\textrm{d}\textbf{W}_t \end{aligned}$$\end{document}dXt=f(Xt,t)dt+g(t)dWtwith the initial condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{X}_0 = \textbf{X} $$\end{document}X0=X, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{W}_t $$\end{document}Wt is the standard Wiener process [56], \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{f}: \mathbb {R}^d \times \mathbb {R}_+ \rightarrow \mathbb {R}^d$$\end{document}f:Rd×R+→Rd is the drift term, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ g: \mathbb {R}_+ \rightarrow \mathbb {R} $$\end{document}g:R+→R is the diffusion term. For parameter identification, the close relation9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {E}[\textbf{X}_0 \mid \textbf{X}_t = \textbf{x}] = \textbf{x} + 2t\nabla \log p_{\textbf{X}_t}(\textbf{x}), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E[X0∣Xt=x]=x+2t∇logpXt(x),between the score of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{\textbf{X}_t}$$\end{document}pXt and the conditional expectation of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{X}_0$$\end{document}X0 given that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{X}_t$$\end{document}Xt takes on a certain value, known as Tweedie’s formula [61, 62] is crucial. We provide a full derivation in Appendix B. This relationship provides a natural way to estimate the parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \theta \in \Theta $$\end{document}θ∈Θ, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta $$\end{document}Θ denotes a possibly constrained parameter set, of a model \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ p_\theta (\,\cdot \,, t) \approx p_{\textbf{X}_t}$$\end{document}pθ(·,t)≈pXt via an MMSE denoising objective for all times \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ t > 0$$\end{document}t>0,10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \min _{\theta \in \Theta } \int _0^{\infty } \mathbb {E}_{(\textbf{x}_0,\textbf{x}_t) \sim p_{\textbf{X}_0,\textbf{X}_t}}\left[ \Vert \textbf{x}_0 - \textbf{x}_t - 2t\nabla \log p_{\theta }(\textbf{x}_t,t)\Vert ^2\right] \textrm{d}t. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}minθ∈Θ∫0∞E(x0,xt)∼pX0,Xt‖x0-xt-2t∇logpθ(xt,t)‖2dt.This objective is commonly referred to as denoising score matching [55, 63] (see [64, Section 3.1] for a derivation from Tweedie’s formula). Sampling from the joint distribution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ p_{\textbf{X}_0,\textbf{X}_t} $$\end{document}pX0,Xt at time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ t $$\end{document}t is achieved via ancestral sampling: The joint distribution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ p_{\textbf{X}_0,\textbf{X}_t} $$\end{document}pX0,Xt factorizes as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{\textbf{X}_t|\textbf{X}_0}p_{\textbf{X}_0} $$\end{document}pXt|X0pX0, where drawing a sample \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{x}_0 \sim p_{\textbf{X}_0} $$\end{document}x0∼pX0 amounts to selecting a random training example. A sample \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{x}_t \sim p_{\textbf{X}_t|\textbf{X}_0=\textbf{x}_0} $$\end{document}xt∼pXt|X0=x0 is then constructed by adding appropriately scaled noise \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{x}_t = \textbf{x}_0 + \sqrt{2t}\textbf{z} $$\end{document}xt=x0+2tz with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{z} \sim N_{0,\textbf{I}} $$\end{document}z∼N0,I to the training example.

Given access to the learned approximate score \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla \log p_\theta $$\end{document}∇logpθ, one can discretize the reverse SDE (8), e.g., by using the Euler–Maruyama scheme [56], and approximately sample from the learned density. In practice, however, the reverse scheme is problematic as no limiting marginal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{X}_\infty $$\end{document}X∞ exists. A common workaround is to set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ p_{\textbf{X}_T} = N_{0, \sigma _{\text {max}\textbf{I}}} $$\end{document}pXT=N0,σmaxI, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \sigma _{\text {max}} $$\end{document}σmax is the highest noise level in the diffusion process.

Inverse problems with diffusion priors

To solve inverse problems in the diffusion framework, the reverse diffusion process stated in (8) has to be conditioned on the acquired measurements. Accordingly, the score of the distribution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ p_{\textbf{X}_t} $$\end{document}pXt is replaced by score of the conditional distribution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ p_{\textbf{X}_t|\textbf{Y}} $$\end{document}pXt|Y. In detail, the reverse-time SDE then is11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{d}\textbf{X}_t = \sqrt{2}\textrm{d}\textbf{W}_t - 2\nabla \log p_{\textbf{X}_t\mid \textbf{Y}}(\textbf{X}_t\mid \textbf{y})\textrm{d}t, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}dXt=2dWt-2∇logpXt∣Y(Xt∣y)dt,with a suitable initial condition for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{X}_T $$\end{document}XT. Using Bayes’ rule, this can be equivalently expressed as12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{d}\textbf{X}_t = \sqrt{2}\textrm{d}\textbf{W}_t - 2\left( \nabla \log p_{\textbf{Y}\mid \textbf{X}_t}(\textbf{y}\mid \textbf{X}_t) + \nabla \log p_{\textbf{X}_t}(\textbf{X}_t)\right) \textrm{d}t, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}dXt=2dWt-2∇logpY∣Xt(y∣Xt)+∇logpXt(Xt)dt,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \nabla \log p_{\textbf{Y}\mid \textbf{X}_t} $$\end{document}∇logpY∣Xt is the score of the time-dependent likelihood and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ p_{\textbf{X}_t} $$\end{document}pXt the score of the time-dependent prior, which can be approximated by the score of the learned parametric distribution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ p_\theta (\,\cdot \,,t) $$\end{document}pθ(·,t). However, the time-dependent likelihood of the measurement that can be written as13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int p_{\textbf{Y}\mid \textbf{X}_0}(\textbf{y}\mid \textbf{X}_0)p_{\textbf{X}_0\mid \textbf{X}_t}(\textbf{X}_0 \mid \textbf{X}_t)\textrm{d}\textbf{X}_0 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫pY∣X0(y∣X0)pX0∣Xt(X0∣Xt)dX0is generally intractable in high-dimensional settings, as it requires marginalization over all possible \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{X}_0 $$\end{document}X0. Consequently, the likelihood term has to be approximated for all times \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ t > 0$$\end{document}t>0. In this work, we use the simple approximation with the choice \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{\textbf{X}_0\mid \textbf{X}_t}(\textbf{X}_0\mid \textbf{X}_t) = \delta (\textbf{X}_0 - \textbf{X}_t)$$\end{document}pX0∣Xt(X0∣Xt)=δ(X0-Xt) where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta $$\end{document}δ is the Dirac measure. The score of the likelihood for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t$$\end{document}t is then14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \nabla \log p_{\textbf{Y}\mid \textbf{X}_t}(\textbf{y}\mid \textbf{X}_t) \approx \nabla \log p_{\textbf{Y}\mid \textbf{X}_0}(\textbf{y} \mid \textbf{X}_t). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∇logpY∣Xt(y∣Xt)≈∇logpY∣X0(y∣Xt).Under the assumption of additive Gaussian noise, the time-conditional likelihood approximation becomes15\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \nabla \log p_{\textbf{Y}\mid \textbf{X}_0=\textbf{x}}(\textbf{y}) \propto -\textbf{J}_\textbf{A}(\textbf{x})^H(\textbf{A}(\textbf{x}) - \textbf{y}) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∇logpY∣X0=x(y)∝-JA(x)H(A(x)-y)where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{J}_{\textbf{A}}(\textbf{x}) \in \mathbb {C}^{d \times m} $$\end{document}JA(x)∈Cd×m is the Jacobian of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{A}$$\end{document}A at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{x}$$\end{document}x and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H$$\end{document}H denotes the Hermitian transpose. This approximation was proposed in [27] for linear \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{A}$$\end{document}A in the context of MRI reconstruction. Another popular alternative to this simple approximation was proposed in [65]. Here the authors choose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{\textbf{X}_0\mid \textbf{X}_t}(\textbf{X}_0\mid \textbf{X}_t) = \delta (\textbf{X}_0 - \mathbb {E}[\textbf{X}_0\mid \textbf{X}_t])$$\end{document}pX0∣Xt(X0∣Xt)=δ(X0-E[X0∣Xt]), which leads to the approximation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \nabla \log p_{\textbf{Y}\mid \textbf{X}_t}(\textbf{X}_t\mid \textbf{y}) \approx \nabla \log p_{\textbf{Y}\mid \textbf{X}_0}(\textbf{y}\mid \mathbb {E}[\textbf{X}_0\mid \textbf{X}_t])$$\end{document}∇logpY∣Xt(Xt∣y)≈∇logpY∣X0(y∣E[X0∣Xt]). The specific choices of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{\textbf{X}_0\mid \textbf{X}_t}(\textbf{X}_0\mid \textbf{X}_t) $$\end{document}pX0∣Xt(X0∣Xt) and different approaches to the time-dependent likelihood approximation are subject to ongoing research. We refer to [47] for a comprehensive overview.

Product-of-Gaussian-mixture diffusion model

In this section, we detail the PoGMDM, describe its time adaptation mechanism and explain why tractable diffusion of this model is unattainable in practice. The PoGMDM models the time-dependent distribution of images \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{x} \in \mathbb {R}^d $$\end{document}x∈Rd as16\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} p_{\theta }(\textbf{x}, t) \propto \prod _{l=1}^{d} \prod _{k=1}^o \psi _k((\textbf{K}_k \textbf{x})_{l}, \textbf{w}_k, t), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}pθ(x,t)∝∏l=1d∏k=1oψk((Kkx)l,wk,t),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{K}_1,...,\textbf{K}_o \in \mathbb {R}^{d \times d}$$\end{document}K1,...,Ko∈Rd×d are convolution matrices2 and the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k$$\end{document}k-th factor17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \psi _k(x, \textbf{w}, t) = \sum _{i=1}^L \frac{\textbf{w}_{i}}{\sqrt{2\pi \sigma _k^2(t)}} \exp \left( -\frac{(x - \mu _i)^2}{2\sigma _k^2(t)} \right) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ψk(x,w,t)=∑i=1Lwi2πσk2(t)exp-(x-μi)22σk2(t)is a one-dimensional Gaussian mixture with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L$$\end{document}L components, weights \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{w} \in \mathbb {R}^L$$\end{document}w∈RL, means \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mu _1, \,...\, \mu _L \in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}μ1,...μL∈R that are shared across the potentials and variance \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _k^2 \in \mathbb {R_{++}}$$\end{document}σk2∈R++ that is shared across the mixture components.

This model admits several advantages compared to the networks that typically serve as the backbone in diffusion models: The number of learnable parameters is small, the shallow architecture enables fast inference, the derived score is a conservative vector field, and, as we show later, the time conditioning adapts the variances \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \sigma _1^2,\dots ,\sigma _o^2 $$\end{document}σ12,⋯,σo2 based on the underlying SDE. However, these advantages come at the cost of limited model capacity and substantially inferior empirical performance compared to typical networks when used as priors in the resolution of inverse problems (see, e.g., Sect. 5.2). The learnable parameters of the model detailed in (16) and (17) are the filters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{f}_k \in \mathbb {R}^r $$\end{document}fk∈Rr of the convolution matrices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{K}_k $$\end{document}Kk and the weights \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{w}_k$$\end{document}wk of the Gaussian mixture factors \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi _k$$\end{document}ψk for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k=1,\dots ,o$$\end{document}k=1,⋯,o. These parameters are learned by minimizing the objective in (10). All other parameters are chosen a priori and fixed (see Sect. 4.3 for parameterization and learning details).

Zach et al. [36] showed that if the convolution matrices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{K}_1,\dots ,\textbf{K}_o$$\end{document}K1,⋯,Ko implement convolutions with circular boundary conditions and ideal filters3 with non-overlapping supports, the convolution of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ p_\theta (\, \cdot \,,0) $$\end{document}pθ(·,0) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ N_{0, 2tI} $$\end{document}N0,2tI in (6) can be implemented solely by a proper adaption of the variances \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma ^2_1,\dots ,\sigma ^2_o$$\end{document}σ12,⋯,σo2 of the one-dimensional Gaussian mixtures \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi _1,\dots ,\psi _o$$\end{document}ψ1,⋯,ψo. In particular, if the variance of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k$$\end{document}k-th factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \psi _k $$\end{document}ψk is adapted as18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sigma _k^2(t) = \sigma _0^2 + \nu _k^2 2t, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}σk2(t)=σ02+νk22t,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \sigma _0^2 $$\end{document}σ02 is some initial variance (available as a modeling choice) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \nu _k \in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}νk∈R is the magnitude spectrum of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k$$\end{document}k-th filter, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_\theta (\, \cdot \,,t) = N_{0, 2t\textbf{I}} * p_\theta (\, \cdot \,,0)$$\end{document}pθ(·,t)=N0,2tI∗pθ(·,0) for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ t > 0 $$\end{document}t>0.

The proof of the time conditioning [36, Theorem 5] relies on the assumption of ideal filters which is unattainable in practice as an ideal filters has infinite spatial support. Consequently, the evolution of the model distribution according to (5) is infeasible. As a practical compromise between filters with local spatial support and ideal one, Zach et al. [36] propose to use nonseparable shearlets [67]. They naturally partition the frequency plane into cones determined by shearing and scaling parameters (see Sect. 4.3 for details). Nevertheless, they violate the ideal filter assumption.

This naturally raises the question of which filters and corresponding time adaption of the variances are optimal in a practical sense. We attempt to answer this in Sect. 3.2, by allowing more general filters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{f}_k$$\end{document}fk and parameterizations of the the map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t \mapsto \sigma _k^2(t)$$\end{document}t↦σk2(t) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k=1,\dots ,o$$\end{document}k=1,⋯,o.

Methods

In this section, we introduce the acquisition model we use for PI-MRI, specify our approximation for the time-dependent prior and likelihood and discuss the employed model parameterizations.

Acquisition model and reconstruction algorithm

We use the SENSE acquisition model that relates the measured data \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \end{pmatrix} + \textbf{n} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}y=A(x,s)+n=MF(s1⊙x)⋮MF(sc⊙x)+nand jointly reconstruct the unknown image \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} the number of measured spatial frequencies. The nonlinear forward operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{A}: \mathbb {C}^{d} \times \mathbb {C}^{d \times c} \rightarrow \mathbb {C}^{e \times c}$$\end{document}A:Cd×Cd×c→Ce×c models an element-wise multiplication between the image \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{x}$$\end{document}x and the coil sensitivities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{s}_1,\dots ,\textbf{s}_c$$\end{document}s1,⋯,sc, denoted as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \odot $$\end{document}⊙, followed by a discrete Fourier transform \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{F}: \mathbb {C}^{d} \rightarrow \mathbb {C}^{d} $$\end{document}F:Cd→Cd and a binary sampling operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{M} \in \mathbb {C}^{e \times d} $$\end{document}M∈Ce×d. The noise term \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{n} \in \mathbb {C}^{e \times c} $$\end{document}n∈Ce×c is modeled as additive complex Gaussian noise.

Recovering \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{x} $$\end{document}x and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{s} $$\end{document}s from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{y} $$\end{document}y is ill-posed even in the fully sampled case (i.e., when the sampling operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{M}$$\end{document}M is the identity), due to a scaling ambiguity [68]: For any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{b} \in \mathbb {C}^{d} $$\end{document}b∈Cd, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ (\textbf{x}^*, \textbf{s}^*) $$\end{document}(x∗,s∗) is a solution to the recovery problem, then so is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ (\textbf{x}^* \odot \textbf{b}, \textbf{s}^* \oslash \textbf{b}) $$\end{document}(x∗⊙b,s∗⊘b), where the symbol \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\oslash $$\end{document}⊘ denotes element-wise division.

We address this problem in the diffusion framework, and introduce a time-dependent prior \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{\textbf{X}_t, \mathbf {\Sigma }_t}$$\end{document}pXt,Σt on the joint distribution of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{X}_t $$\end{document}Xt and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mathbf {\Sigma }_t $$\end{document}Σt. Here \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{X}_t $$\end{document}Xt denotes the random variable of the image, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mathbf {\Sigma }_t $$\end{document}Σt the random variable of the coil sensitivities, both of which indexed with the diffusion time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t$$\end{document}t. From the forward model in (19), we derive the likelihood \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ p_{\textbf{Y}|\textbf{X}_t,\mathbf {\Sigma }_t}(\textbf{y} \mid \textbf{x}, \textbf{s}) \propto \exp \left( -\tfrac{1}{2}\Vert \textbf{A}(\textbf{x},\textbf{s}) - \textbf{y}\Vert _2^2\right) $$\end{document}pY|Xt,Σt(y∣x,s)∝exp-12‖A(x,s)-y‖22, where we make use of the approximation discussed in Sect. 2.3. For a given datum \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{Y} = \textbf{y}$$\end{document}Y=y, we denote the time-dependent posterior as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} p_{\textbf{X}_t,\mathbf {\Sigma }_t\mid \textbf{Y}}(\textbf{x}, \textbf{s} \mid \textbf{y})&\propto p_{\textbf{Y}|\textbf{X}_t,\mathbf {\Sigma }_t}(\textbf{y} \mid \textbf{x}, \textbf{s}) \, p_{\textbf{X}_t,\mathbf {\Sigma }_t}(\textbf{x}, \textbf{s}) \\&= p_{\textbf{Y}|\textbf{X}_t,\mathbf {\Sigma }_t}(\textbf{y} \mid \textbf{x}, \textbf{s}) \, p_{\textbf{X}_t}(\textbf{x}) \, p_{\mathbf {\Sigma }_t}(\textbf{s}), \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}pXt,Σt∣Y(x,s∣y)∝pY|Xt,Σt(y∣x,s)pXt,Σt(x,s)=pY|Xt,Σt(y∣x,s)pXt(x)pΣt(s),where we make use of Bayes’ theorem and the simplifying assumption that the joint distribution of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ (\textbf{X}_t, \mathbf {\Sigma }_t) $$\end{document}(Xt,Σt) factorizes. Although this factorization neglects correlations between coil sensitivities and the scanned anatomy, it has been observed to work well in practice [6, 35]. For the time-dependent prior on the image \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ p_{\textbf{X}_t}$$\end{document}pXt, we use the learned PoGMDM \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ p_{\theta }(\, \cdot \,, t) $$\end{document}pθ(·,t) trained on clinically available magnitude reference reconstructions. Note that this introduces a slight mismatch between the random variable \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{X}_t $$\end{document}Xt which takes valued in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mathbb {C}^d $$\end{document}Cd and the prior that is learned on real-valued signals. To address this, we follow the heuristic proposed in [28] and apply the prior separately to the real- and imaginary-parts of the image. For the prior on the coil sensitivities, we follow [6] who choose a classical smoothness prior \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ p_{\mathbf {\Sigma }_t}(\textbf{s}) \propto \exp (-\gamma (\textbf{s})) $$\end{document}pΣt(s)∝exp(-γ(s)), with20\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \gamma :\mathbb {C}^{d \times c} \rightarrow \mathbb {R}: \textbf{s} \mapsto \frac{1}{2}\sum _{i=1}^{c} \left( \Vert \textbf{D}\text {Re}(\textbf{s}_{i})\Vert _2^2 + \Vert \textbf{D}\text {Im}(\textbf{s}_{i})\Vert _2^2\right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}γ:Cd×c→R:s↦12∑i=1c‖DRe(si)‖22+‖DIm(si)‖22,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{D}:\mathbb {R}^{d} \rightarrow \mathbb {R}^{2d} $$\end{document}D:Rd→R2d is a forward-finite-differences operator with Dirichlet boundary conditions that ensure that the coil sensitivities are zero outside the image domain [6]. For reconstruction, we follow [28] and use the predictor–corrector algorithm introduced in [55] to solve the reverse SDE. Here the predictor implements an Euler–Maruyama discretization of the reverse-time SDE and the corrector applies annealed Langevin dynamics [53]. For the predictor, we adopt the discretization schedule \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \zeta (t) = \zeta _{\textrm{max}}(\zeta _{\textrm{min}}/\zeta _{\textrm{max}})^{(1 - t/T)^p} $$\end{document}ζ(t)=ζmax(ζmin/ζmax)p where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ t \in [0, T] $$\end{document}t∈[0,T] and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ p \in \mathbb {R_+} $$\end{document}p∈R+ (see Appendix A for more details). In each step of the reverse SDE, data consistency is enforced by doing gradient descent steps, where the gradient of the log-likelihood w.r.t. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{x} $$\end{document}x for fixed coil sensitivities is21\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \nabla _{\textbf{x}} \log p_{\textbf{Y}|\textbf{X}_t, \mathbf {\Sigma }_t}(\textbf{y}\mid \textbf{x}, \textbf{s}) \propto \sum _{i=1}^{c} \bar{\textbf{s}}_{i} \odot (\textbf{F}^H\textbf{M}^H(\textbf{y}_i - \textbf{M}\textbf{F}(\textbf{s}_{i} \odot \textbf{x}))), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∇xlogpY|Xt,Σt(y∣x,s)∝∑i=1cs¯i⊙(FHMH(yi-MF(si⊙x))),where the overline denotes complex conjugation. For updating the coil sensitivities, we found it sufficient to fix the prior for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ t > 0 $$\end{document}t>0. In this step, we depart from the standard diffusion framework and instead perform proximal-gradient updates on the sensitivities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{s} $$\end{document}s. The proximal map of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ -\log p_{\mathbf {\Sigma }_t} $$\end{document}-logpΣt admits the closed-form solution22\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {prox}_{\mu \gamma }(\textbf{s}) = \begin{pmatrix} Q_\mu (\text {Re}(\textbf{s}_{1})) + iQ_\mu (\text {Im}(\textbf{s}_{1})) \\ \vdots \\ Q_\mu (\text {Re}(\textbf{s}_{c})) + iQ_\mu (\text {Im}(\textbf{s}_{c})) \end{pmatrix}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}proxμγ(s)=Qμ(Re(s1))+iQμ(Im(s1))⋮Qμ(Re(sc))+iQμ(Im(sc)),with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mu \in \mathbb {R}_{+} $$\end{document}μ∈R+ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ Q_\mu :\mathbb {R}^{d} \rightarrow \mathbb {R}^{d}; \textbf{x} \mapsto \textbf{S}^T(\textbf{S}(\mu \textbf{x}) \odot (\boldsymbol{\tau } + \mu )^{-1})$$\end{document}Qμ:Rd→Rd;x↦ST(S(μx)⊙(τ+μ)-1). Here, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{S}: \mathbb {R}^{d \times d} $$\end{document}S:Rd×d is the two-dimensional discrete sine transform, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \boldsymbol{\tau } $$\end{document}τ are the eigenvalues of the two-dimensional discrete Laplace operator [69]. The application of this proximal operator in each step of the reverse diffusion ensures smoothness of the coil sensitivities. The gradient of the log-likelihood w.r.t. to the coil sensitivities is23\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \nabla _{\textbf{s}} \log p_{\textbf{Y}|\textbf{X}_t,\mathbf {\Sigma }_t}(\textbf{y}\mid \textbf{x}, \textbf{s}) = \begin{pmatrix} \textbf{x} \odot (\textbf{F}^H\textbf{M}^H(\textbf{y}_1 - \textbf{M}\textbf{F}(\textbf{s}_{1} \odot \textbf{x}))) \\ \vdots \\ \textbf{x} \odot (\textbf{F}^H\textbf{M}^H(\textbf{y}_c - \textbf{M}\textbf{F}(\textbf{s}_{c} \odot \textbf{x}))) \end{pmatrix}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∇slogpY|Xt,Σt(y∣x,s)=x⊙(FHMH(y1-MF(s1⊙x)))⋮x⊙(FHMH(yc-MF(sc⊙x))).

The step size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek}
