Finally, we want to stress that the resulting algorithm departs significantly from the resolution of the conditional reverse diffusion. Due to the coil sensitivity updates and approximate data consistency, the approach is closer to a stochastic graduated-nonconvexity heuristic than a rigorous probabilistic resolution of the problem.

Extended PoGMDM parameterization

To balance theoretical assumptions with practical utility of the filters, the original PoGMDM formulation [36] employs nonseparable shearlets. In this work, we explore two alternative parameterizations: (i) We parameterize the filters as fully learnable while retaining the original time conditioning detailed in (18), and (ii) additionally learn the time conditioning directly from data. As we cannot assume ideal filters, the scaling for the diffusion time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu _k^2$$\end{document}νk2 in (18) has to be calculated with a heuristic. Here, we follow [36] and set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \nu _k^2 = \Vert |\textbf{F}\textbf{f}_k|\Vert _\infty $$\end{document}νk2=‖|Ffk|‖∞. We also experimented with an alternative heuristic given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \nu _k^2 = \tfrac{1}{b}\sum _b(|\textbf{F}\textbf{f}_k|)_b$$\end{document}νk2=1b∑b(|Ffk|)b but observed no significant difference. In (ii) we adapt the variances of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k$$\end{document}k-th one-dimensional Gaussian mixture factor as24\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sigma ^2_k(t) = \sigma _0^2 + (\tau _{\theta }(t))_k \end{aligned}$$\end{document}σk2(t)=σ02+(τθ(t))kwith the diffusion time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ t $$\end{document}t for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k = 1,\dots ,o$$\end{document}k=1,⋯,o. Here, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \tau _\theta : \mathbb {R} \rightarrow \mathbb {R}^o_+ $$\end{document}τθ:R→R+o maps the time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ t $$\end{document}t to an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ o $$\end{document}o-dimensional positive vector. We parameterize \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \tau _\theta $$\end{document}τθ as the map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ t \mapsto (\text {softplus} \circ L_3 \circ \text {ELU} \circ L_2 \circ \text {ELU} \circ L_1)(\sqrt{2t}) $$\end{document}t↦(softplus∘L3∘ELU∘L2∘ELU∘L1)(2t), with linear layers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ L_i: x \mapsto W_ix + b_i,\, i = 1,2,3$$\end{document}Li:x↦Wix+bi,i=1,2,3 and the exponential linear unit (ELU) function with unit scaling. The final \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \text {softplus}: x \mapsto \ln (1 + \exp (x))$$\end{document}softplus:x↦ln(1+exp(x)) ensures that the outputs are strictly positive. By learning the time conditioning from data, the model partially compensates for empirical diffusion inaccuracies in the original PoGMDM. This approach also enables the dynamic disabling of filters with diffusion time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t$$\end{document}t (see Sect. 5.1 for a detailed discussion).

Numerical experiments

In this section, we first describe the data used in our experiments. We then outline the experimental setup, including the parameter settings of the joint reconstruction algorithm and the evaluation metrics. Finally, we provide learning details for our used models.

Experimental data

We use the fastMRI dataset [70] for training, hyperparameter search, and evaluation. The training data consist of root-sum-of-squares reconstructions of the central eleven slices of the CORPD training split to avoid training on noise-only data. This yields 5324 training images of size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$320 \times 320$$\end{document}320×320 which we normalize with the map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{x} \mapsto \textbf{x} / \Vert \textbf{x}\Vert _{\infty }$$\end{document}x↦x/‖x‖∞.

The CORPD validation split is divided into 30 validation and 58 test files, excluding k-spaces with width different from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$368$$\end{document}368 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$372$$\end{document}372 to balance dataset size and computational cost. To test the robustness of our reconstruction algorithm on contrast mechanism out-of-distribution data, we conduct experiments with the CORPDFS knee validation split. We apply the same exclusion criteria as for the CORPD dataset, which results in 30 validation and 69 test files. For anatomical out-of-distribution experiments, we use contrast-agent T1- and T2-weighted brain scans from the fastMRI brain dataset [70]. We exclude k-space data with width different from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ 320 $$\end{document}320 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$322$$\end{document}322 and restrict our experiments to data with four coils to manage the computational cost of the experiments. This yields 20 validation and 43 test files for T1 and 20 validation and 52 test files for T2.

Experimental setup

The reconstruction algorithm is evaluated in two experiments: (i) a real-valued single-coil simulation with synthetic data, and (ii) joint PI reconstruction on both in-distribution (CORPD) and out-of-distribution (CORPDFS, T1 brain and T2 brain) datasets. Details for the experiments and the comparison methods are provided in the respective sections presenting the numerical results.

We also conduct a concise ablation study of the PoGMDM parameterizations for the shearlet model and the learned time conditioning. For all joint reconstruction experiments, we report peak signal-to-noise ratio (PSNR), normalized mean squared error (NMSE), and structural similarity (SSIM) of the magnitude of the reconstructed image relative to the reference RSS reconstructions. Unless stated otherwise, we obtain and report Monte Carlo estimates of the MMSE estimate of the diffusion posterior by averaging 25 independent reconstructions.

To ensure a fair comparison, our method includes an additional post-processing step to match the intensity distribution of the reference RSS reconstructions. Following [17], we correct small intensity shifts by multiplying the final reconstruction with the RSS of the estimated coil sensitivities:25\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textbf{x} \mapsto \textbf{x} \cdot \sqrt{\sum _{i=1}^{c} |\textbf{s}_i|^2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}x↦x·∑i=1c|si|2.Similar to [6], we fit a spline curve against the empirical scatter of reconstructed and reference intensities to adjust background intensities.

For all experiments, the parameters in Algorithm 1 are set to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N=1000$$\end{document}N=1000, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M=1$$\end{document}M=1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda =1$$\end{document}λ=1. We find an optimal step size parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r$$\end{document}r and trade-off parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ for each k-space trajectory with grid search. For the noise schedule in (A1), we set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta _{\textrm{max}} = 10$$\end{document}ζmax=10, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta _{\textrm{min}} = 0.001$$\end{document}ζmin=0.001, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p=5$$\end{document}p=5 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T=1$$\end{document}T=1 in all experiments. Finally, we use the acceleration method proposed in [71] with vanilla initialization and a reduced reverse diffusion starting time of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_0=0.2 T$$\end{document}t0=0.2T.

Learning details

In this section, we provide parameterization- and learning details of the models used in our experiments.

Filters selection

For the shearlet PoGMDM, we largely follow [36] and use nonseparable shearlet filters. These filters are constructed by specifying, a scaling \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ j > 0 $$\end{document}j>0, translations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ m \in \mathbb {Z} $$\end{document}m∈Z, and a range of shearings \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ |k| \le \big \lceil 2^{\lfloor j/2 \rfloor } \big \rceil $$\end{document}|k|≤⌈2⌊j/2⌋⌉. The maximum number of shearings per scale is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ 2 |k| + 1 $$\end{document}2|k|+1. In all experiments, we use the maximal possible number of shearings per scale, e.g., a scaling of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ j=2 $$\end{document}j=2 leads to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ 5 $$\end{document}5 shearings per scale. Considering both the vertical and horizontal cones, this yields \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ o = 2 \times 5 \times 2 = 20 $$\end{document}o=2×5×2=20. The shearlet system itself is constructed from a one-dimensional low-pass filter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{h}_1 $$\end{document}h1 and a two-dimensional directional filter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{P} $$\end{document}P, both of which are learned during training unless specified otherwise. We use the same initialization for the filters as in [36] and ensure that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{h}_1 $$\end{document}h1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{P} $$\end{document}P define a valid shearlet system by projecting them onto suitable constraints during their iterative numerical optimization, see the details in [36].

For the model whose filters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{f}_1,\dots ,\textbf{f}_o \in \mathbb {R}^{v \times v}$$\end{document}f1,⋯,fo∈Rv×v are fully learnable, we set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v = 5$$\end{document}v=5, and initialize the weights using the normal distribution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_{0, \frac{1}{ov^2}\textbf{I}}$$\end{document}N0,1ov2I. Like in [72], we enforce that the mean of the filters is zero to ensure invariance to radiometric shifts. This prevents the model from responding to global intensity biases often found in MRI images [73].

Gaussian mixture factors

For the parameterization of the one-dimensional Gaussian mixture factors, we follow [36] and use \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ L = 125 $$\end{document}L=125 components whose means are spaced equidistantly over a predefined interval \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[v_{\text {min}}, v_{\text {max}}]$$\end{document}[vmin,vmax]. Specifically, we set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{\text {min}}=-0.5$$\end{document}vmin=-0.5 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{\text {max}}=0.5$$\end{document}vmax=0.5 for all shearlet models and the model with learned time conditioning. For the model with fully learned filters and the original time conditioning, we found it beneficial to set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{\text {min}}=-1$$\end{document}vmin=-1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{\text {max}}=1$$\end{document}vmax=1. The mixture weights are constrained to the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ L $$\end{document}L-dimensional unit simplex, and symmetry is enforced by mirroring the first \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \lceil L/2 \rceil - 1 $$\end{document}⌈L/2⌉-1 entries of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{w}_k $$\end{document}wk for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k = 1,\dots ,o$$\end{document}k=1,⋯,o around zero. Moreover, all Gaussian mixture components share a common initial variance \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \sigma _0^2 = (v_{\text {max}} - v_{\text {min}}) / (L -1) $$\end{document}σ02=(vmax-vmin)/(L-1).

Parameter identification

For parameter identification, we minimize the objective stated in (10) using the AdaBelief optimizer [74], complemented by projection steps to enforce the parameter constraints discussed above. Similar to [55], we approximate the expectation over the time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ t $$\end{document}t in the interval \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ [0, T] $$\end{document}[0,T] by sampling from a uniform distribution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ t \sim U_{0,T} $$\end{document}t∼U0,T with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ T=1 $$\end{document}T=1. We train for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ 100\,000 $$\end{document}100000 iterations with parameter-group-specific learning rates depending on the chosen model parameterization. Furthermore, we apply an exponential moving average of the parameters with a momentum factor of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ 0.999 $$\end{document}0.999.

Results and discussion

We first visualize the potentials and the filters of the learned PoGMDMs and provide a detailed discussion of the variant which implements the learned time conditioning in Sect. 5.1. Then, in Sect. 5.2, we report quantitative results for the real-valued single-coil simulation with synthetic data. Finally, we show numerical results for PI on both in-distribution, contrast, and anatomical out-of-distribution data in Sect. 5.3.

Learned models

Shearlets

Fig. 1Learned shearlet model with Gaussian mixture factors and two shearlet scales. The first block shows the spectra of the learned shearlet filters. In the second block, the first row shows the vertical cone and the second row the horizontal cone of the shearlet system. The first five entries correspond to the shearing for the first scale and the second five for the second scale. The potentials and the negative logarithm of the empirical response marginals are shown for different diffusion times

Learned shearlet model with Gaussian mixture factors and two shearlet scales. The first block shows the spectra of the learned shearlet filters. In the second block, the first row shows the vertical cone and the second row the horizontal cone of the shearlet system. The first five entries correspond to the shearing for the first scale and the second five for the second scale. The potentials and the negative logarithm of the empirical response marginals are shown for different diffusion times

We show the learned filters implemented by the convolution matrices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{K}_1,\dots , \textbf{K}_o $$\end{document}K1,⋯,Ko and their corresponding potentials \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$- \log \psi _1(\,\cdot \,, \textbf{w}_1, t), \dots , - \log \psi _o(\,\cdot \,, \textbf{w}_o, t) $$\end{document}-logψ1(·,w1,t),⋯,-logψo(·,wo,t) for different diffusion times in Fig. 1. Due to the overcompleteness of the model—i.e., the fact that the number of filter responses \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d \cdot o$$\end{document}d·o exceeds the input dimension \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d$$\end{document}d—the learned potentials do not resemble the marginal distributions of the filter responses (see, e.g., the empirical response marginals in Fig. 1). Instead, they are more complexly shaped and have multiple local minima, sometimes different from zero (e.g., the first and last potential in the vertical cone of the second scale in Fig. 1). This enables the amplification of specific structures in the images when employing this diffusion prior [36]. The authors of [75, 76] report similar observations. Furthermore, the potentials associated with filters at scaling \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ j=1 $$\end{document}j=1 show smaller variations compared to those at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ j=2 $$\end{document}j=2. This can be attributed to the higher magnitude maximum in the filter spectra, which in turn leads to stronger variation with the diffusion time (see (18)). Similar to [36], the spectra are overlapping and non-constant on their support, which shows that the theoretical assumptions discussed in Sect. 2.4 are not met.

Interestingly, the learned potentials exhibit symmetries both within cones (e.g., the first and fifth potential in the first row) and across cones (e.g., potentials between rows) for a given scaling \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ j $$\end{document}j. These symmetries likely arise from the structure of the training data which is evident in the empirical response marginals in Fig. 1. They suggest that the number of parameters in the model can be reduced without sacrificing its expressiveness. We present numerical results supporting this observation in Sect. 5.4.

Fully learned filters

We show in Fig. 2 the learned filters and their associated potentials. Some of the learned filters resemble classical ones: For example, the eighth filter in the first row and the sixth filter in the second row resemble second-order derivative filters in the vertical and horizontal directions. The learned potentials again exhibit complex shapes due to overcompleteness. Interestingly, unlike in the shearlet model, some potentials show sharp peaks at zero (e.g., the fifth and eighth potential in the bottom row of Fig. 2). Similar to the shearlet model, we observe that some of the learned potentials only show small variation with diffusion time, while others show stronger variations, which is again a consequence of the strong variation in the maximum of the magnitude spectra of the learned filters.Fig. 2Learned model with Gaussian mixture factors and fully learned zero-mean filters of size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(5 \times 5)$$\end{document}(5×5). The variances of the Gaussian mixture factors evolve with diffusion time according to (18). The potentials are shown for different diffusion times

Learned model with Gaussian mixture factors and fully learned zero-mean filters of size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(5 \times 5)$$\end{document}(5×5). The variances of the Gaussian mixture factors evolve with diffusion time according to (18). The potentials are shown for different diffusion times

Fully learned filters and time conditioning

So far, we only discussed models that implement the time conditioning detailed in (18), which is motivated by the theory, but effectively acts as a heuristic since we cannot have ideal filters and choose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \nu _k^2 = \Vert |\textbf{F}\textbf{f}_k|\Vert _\infty $$\end{document}νk2=‖|Ffk|‖∞ as a practical workaround. Now, we discuss a model that learns the time conditioning as described in Sect. 3.2. Figure 3 illustrates the learned filters and their corresponding potentials. As in the previous model, several filters resemble classical ones—for instance, the fifth filter in the first row and the fourth filter in the second row resemble finite-difference filters.Fig. 3Learned model with Gaussian mixture factors, fully learned zero-mean filters of size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(5 \times 5)$$\end{document}(5×5) and learned time conditioning. The variances of the Gaussian mixture factors evolve with diffusion time according to (24). The potentials are shown for different diffusion times

Learned model with Gaussian mixture factors, fully learned zero-mean filters of size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(5 \times 5)$$\end{document}(5×5) and learned time conditioning. The variances of the Gaussian mixture factors evolve with diffusion time according to (24). The potentials are shown for different diffusion times

Similar to the model with fully learned convolutional filters and the original time conditioning, some potentials exhibit maxima at zero. However, most of them differ substantially from those obtained using the time conditioning in (18). In particular, certain potentials equilibrate rapidly as diffusion time progresses, while others evolve slowly. Figure 4 shows the output of the learned time-embedding network \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \tau _\theta $$\end{document}τθ for all factors over the interval \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \sqrt{2t} \in [0, 1] $$\end{document}2t∈[0,1]. Here, we observe that most potentials begin to smooth at different diffusion times, with their time conditioning following an approximately linear progression with time. This is especially apparent in the output for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \psi _1 $$\end{document}ψ1 which increases only slightly in the interval \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ [0, 0.2] $$\end{document}[0,0.2], whereas the output for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \psi _2 $$\end{document}ψ2 increases rapidly after approximately \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \sqrt{2t} = 0.1 $$\end{document}2t=0.1. As a result, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ -\log \psi _2(\, \cdot \,, \textbf{w}_k, t) $$\end{document}-logψ2(·,wk,t) is almost constant in the interval \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ [-0.5, 0.5] $$\end{document}[-0.5,0.5] for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \sqrt{2t} = 0.2 $$\end{document}2t=0.2. The right plot shows the potentials (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-\log \psi _{15}$$\end{document}-logψ15 top, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-\log \psi _{2}$$\end{document}-logψ2 bottom) at diffusion times . The corresponding values of the time conditioning are marked in the inset plot with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\times $$\end{document}× in the same colors

More specifically, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ -\log \psi _k(\, \cdot \,, \textbf{w}_k, t) = c $$\end{document}-logψk(·,wk,t)=c for some constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ c \in \mathbb {R}_+ $$\end{document}c∈R+ on a given interval, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \psi _k(\, \cdot \,, \textbf{w}_k, t) = \exp (-c) $$\end{document}ψk(·,wk,t)=exp(-c) on that interval. Hence, the potential is independent of the filter response whenever it lies in this range, and contributes only a multiplicative constant to the product of the experts. Interestingly, the time-embedding network appears to learn to ignore certain filters entirely (e.g., the sixth and ninth filters in the second row of Fig. 3), as also reflected in Fig. 4, where the outputs of these potentials remain substantially above zero even for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \sqrt{2t} = 0 $$\end{document}2t=0.

Figure 5 shows the adaption of the one-dimensional Gaussian mixture factor variances for the model with fully learned filters and the original time conditioning. We immediately observe that—for the choice \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu _k = \Vert \mid \textbf{F}\textbf{f}_k\mid \Vert _\infty $$\end{document}νk=‖∣Ffk∣‖∞—the time conditioning for most of the factors grows very slowly. This explains the observations in Fig. 2, where many of the potentials show little variation with diffusion time. We compare two examples for a slowly and fast changing potential with diffusion time in the right part of Fig. 5.

Finally, we note that the learning of this model is likely suboptimal, as it does not appear to fully exploit its capacity (e.g., see the sixth and ninth potentials in the second row of Fig. 3). Addressing this limitation is left for future work.

Synthetic single-coil data

We now turn to reconstruction results and assess the performance of the models on a synthetic single-coil experiment. To this end, we simulate measurements as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{y} = \textbf{M}\textbf{F}\textbf{x} + \textbf{n}$$\end{document}y=MFx+n, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{x}$$\end{document}x is a normalized CORPD reference RSS reconstruction and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{n} $$\end{document}n is Gaussian noise with standard deviation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \sigma = 0.02 $$\end{document}σ=0.02 added to the synthetic data. To account for this setup in the reconstruction algorithm, we fix the coil sensitivities to the identity and do not update them in the iterations. Furthermore, we only consider the real part of the iterates. We compare our approach against a classical variational penalty, an end-to-end trained baseline and a state-of-the-art diffusion-based approach. As a variational baseline, we consider the Charbonnier \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \varepsilon $$\end{document}ε-smoothed isotropic TV26\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {TV}: \textbf{x} \mapsto \lambda \sum _{i=1}^{d} \sqrt{(\textbf{D}\textbf{x})_{i}^2 + (\textbf{D}\textbf{x})_{i+d}^2 + \varepsilon ^2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}TV:x↦λ∑i=1d(Dx)i2+(Dx)i+d2+ε2.For the end-to-end method, we choose the fastMRI baseline U-Net [70], and for the diffusion-based method, we select scoreMRI [28]. As a reference point, we additionally report results for a naive zero-filled (ZF) reconstruction, where missing k-space data are replaced by zero prior to inverting the Fourier transformation. For both the U-Net and scoreMRI, we use the pretrained models provided by the authors. The parameters for the reverse diffusion process for scoreMRI are set to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N=2000$$\end{document}N=2000, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M=1$$\end{document}M=1, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r=0.16$$\end{document}r=0.16, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _\text {min}=0.01$$\end{document}σmin=0.01 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _\text {max}=378.00$$\end{document}σmax=378.00 following the choices in [28]. Furthermore, we employ the same acceleration technique as described in Sect. 4.2, and set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_0 = 0.2T$$\end{document}t0=0.2T. The optimal regularization parameter for TV is selected for each k-space trajectory using grid search on the validation dataset.

Table 1 shows numerical results for the single-coil experiment with synthetic data for four different k-space trajectories. ScoreMRI beats all comparison method across all k-space trajectories. Here, the gap between our best method and scoreMRI ranges from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0.35 $$\end{document}0.35 dB in the spiral case to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ 1.13 $$\end{document}1.13 dB in the Cartesian case. However, the superior performance of scoreMRI comes at the cost of learning \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ 6.7 \times 10^7 $$\end{document}6.7×107 parameters during training and slow inference. Specifically, our method requires roughly two seconds per reconstruction, compared to approximately 129 s for scoreMRI on the same NVIDIA RTX 4090 GPU. All of our models consistently outperform TV, except for the Gaussian k-space trajectory, where the shearlet model achieves a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ 0.03 $$\end{document}0.03 dB lower PSNR compared to TV. Comparing within our models, both variants with fully learned \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(5 \times 5)$$\end{document}(5×5) filters consistently outperform the shearlet model, achieving improvements in roughly 2 dB in the Cartesian and spiral cases. In the variant without learned time conditioning, this gain comes at the cost of just learning 200 additional parameters. The model that jointly learns the time conditioning and the convolutional filters yields a marginal improvement over the variant with the original time conditioning for the Cartesian and spiral k-space trajectories. The performance is identical for the Gaussian k-space trajectory, and the original time conditioning even slightly outperforms the learned version for the radial k-space trajectory.

Parallel imaging

We now evaluate our approach on real data. We compare against three baselines: (i) the Charbonnier \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon $$\end{document}ε-smoothed isotropic TV embedded in the joint nonlinear inversion algorithm of [6], (ii) the end-to-end variational network (VN) of [77], which extends [5] to joint MRI reconstruction, and (iii) the end-to-end learned joint reconstruction baseline Deep-JSENSE (DJS) [25]. We use the publicly available pretrained weights for the VN, retrain DJS on the same training split as our method (see Appendix D for further details) and determine the optimal regularization parameters for TV for each k-space trajectory using the procedure described in [6]. The diffusion-based method scoreMRI from the synthetic single-coil experiment is not included here, as it does not support arbitrarily sized inputs due to the architecture of the score network, which is required in this experiment. Again, we report the ZF reconstruction as a reference point. Finally, to approximately map the data to intensities matching the training distribution, we normalize the measurements by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{y} \mapsto \textbf{y}/\Vert \textbf{x}_{\text {ZF}}\Vert _\infty $$\end{document}y↦y/‖xZF‖∞ and rescale the final magnitude reconstruction as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{x}_0 \mapsto |\textbf{x}_0|\Vert \textbf{x}_{\text {ZF}}\Vert _\infty $$\end{document}x0↦|x0|‖xZF‖∞. We report the quantitative results for PI reconstruction on the CORPD dataset in Table 2. Our methods consistently outperform TV across all k-space trajectories, achieving improvements of up to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ 3.4 $$\end{document}3.4 dB in the Cartesian case with rotated phase-encoding direction and the case with less available ACLs. The VN achieves the highest performance in its training setting (Cartesian k-space trajectory with 8% ACLs). In this scenario, our best method attains a PSNR 1.42 dB lower.

The performance of the VN deteriorates significantly even under minor deviations from its training setting, for instance when the fraction of ACLs is reduced from 8% to 4%. An example of this is shown in Fig. 6, where the VN struggles to produce artifact-free reconstructions, even in the Cartesian setting, and introduces artifacts in the femur and tibia. For the radial k-space trajectory, these artifacts become more severe and accompanied by hallucinations, as highlighted in the inset of Fig. 6.Fig. 7Qualitative PI comparison on the CORPD dataset against TV. First row: 4-fold Cartesian k-space trajectory with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$8\%$$\end{document}8% available ACLs and an acceleration of 4. Second row: same as row one with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$4\%$$\end{document}4% available ACLs. Third row: same as row one with rotated phase-encoding direction. Fourth row: 2D Gaussian k-space trajectory with an acceleration of 8. Fifth row: radial k-space trajectory with an acceleration of 11. The inset shows a zoom on the image with corresponding absolute error. Qualitative PI comparison on the CORPD dataset against TV. First row: 4-fold Cartesian k-space trajectory with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$8\%$$\end{document}8% available ACLs and an acceleration of 4. Second row: same as row one with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$4\%$$\end{document}4% available ACLs. Third row: same as row one with rotated phase-encoding direction. Fourth row: 2D Gaussian k-space trajectory with an acceleration of 8. Fifth row: radial k-space trajectory with an acceleration of 11. The inset shows a zoom on the image with corresponding absolute error. Our methods: shearlet (SH), fully learned filters (FLF), and fully learned filters and time conditioning (FLF+TC)

Figure 7 shows qualitative results for the in-distribution setting against TV. In the prototypical case of a Cartesian k-space trajectory with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$8 \%$$\end{document}8% available ACLs, the reconstruction obtained with TV appears largely artifact-free, but the method fails to recover fine details. In contrast, the reconstructions obtained with the shearlet model show more small scale details which are also visible in the RSS reference. However, in some reconstructions slight backfolding artifacts, such as those visible in the upper part of the femur, remain. Both models which employ fully learned convolutional filters produce artifact-free reconstructions, and the methods retain even finer details such as the sharper vertical edge visible in the zoomed regions of the two rightmost reconstructions in Fig. 7. With a reduced number of ACLs, the TV reconstruction exhibits pronounced backfolding artifacts in the femur; again our models remain largely robust. Furthermore, for the Cartesian with rotated phase-encoding and radial k-space trajectory, the models with fully learned filters produce less noisy reconstructions and enhance structural details such as vessels in the fat tissue better. For the Gaussian k-space trajectory, all of our methods yield comparable results. This is likely due to the fact that the measured data inherently retain substantial amounts of low- and high-frequency information (as evident by the zero-filled solution which already achieves \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ 32.15 $$\end{document}32.15 dB in Table 2).

DJS outperforms our method in terms of SSIM in both regular Cartesian settings. However, its mean PSNR and NMSE are inferior. Interestingly, DJS demonstrates greater robustness to changes in the forward model compared to the VN. This is both reflected in the radial and the Cartesian k-space trajectory with rotated phase encoding, where DJS significantly outperforms the VN and achieves a SSIM comparable to our method. For the Gaussian k-space trajectory, DJS produces plausible reconstructions but struggles with accurate coil sensitivity estimation. This limitation introduces noticeable artifacts, examples of which are shown in Fig. 8. In contrast, our methods maintain robustness across all variations.

Comparing the shearlet model with the two models with fully learned convolutional filters, we observe that the former is inferior for all considered k-space trajectories. Specifically, the model with additionally learned time conditioning achieves an average improvement of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0.7$$\end{document}0.7 dB over the shearlet model, while outperforming the model with fully learned convolutional filters and the original time conditioning by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0.15$$\end{document}0.15 dB on average.Fig. 8Qualitative PI comparison on the CORPD dataset against DJS for a two-dimensional Gaussian k-space trajectory with and acceleration of 8. DJS introduces contrast artifacts in the lateral fat tissue, due to suboptimal coil sensitivity estimation. In contrast, these are not present in the reconstructions of our models. The inset shows a zoom on the image with corresponding absolute error. Qualitative PI comparison on the CORPD dataset against DJS for a two-dimensional Gaussian k-space trajectory with and acceleration of 8. DJS introduces contrast artifacts in the lateral fat tissue, due to suboptimal coil sensitivity estimation. In contrast, these are not present in the reconstructions of our models. The inset shows a zoom on the image with corresponding absolute error. Our methods: shearlet (SH), fully learned filters (FLF), and fully learned filters and time conditioning (FLF+TC)

Contrast out of distribution

We now investigate the robustness of the methods to shifts in the contrast mechanism, transitioning from CORPD-weighted to CORPDFS-weighted data. We show in Table 3 the quantitative numerical results across different k-space trajectories. Interestingly, due to the shift in the contrast mechanism, the performance of the VN deteriorates even for the vertical Cartesian case with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$8\%$$\end{document}8%ACLs. Similar to the results in the previous section, the performance of the VN further decreases for all other k-space trajectories. DJS shows no robustness to the contrast mechanism shift, with its average performance falling below the naive zero-filled baseline. Furthermore, the high standard deviation across all metrics indicates significant reconstruction variability, i.e., some reconstructions remain plausible, while others are contaminated by severe artifacts. This behavior, along with the high NMSE values, is further investigated in Appendix D. In contrast, TV remains robust and delivers good results across all cases. Among our models, the model which implements fully learned filters performs best across all k-space trajectories and comparison methods, achieving an average improvement of around \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0.45$$\end{document}0.45 dB in terms of PSNR compared to TV.Fig. 9Qualitative PI comparison on the CORPDFS dataset against the VN and TV. First row: 4-fold Cartesian k-space trajectory with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$8\%$$\end{document}8% available ACLs and an acceleration of 4. ACLs. Second row: 4-fold Cartesian k-space trajectory with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$4\%$$\end{document}4% ACLs and an acceleration of 4. The inset shows a zoom on the image with corresponding absolute error. Our methods: fully learned filters (FLF) and fully learned filters and time conditioning (FLF+TC)

Qualitative PI comparison on the CORPDFS dataset against the VN and TV. First row: 4-fold Cartesian k-space trajectory with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$8\%$$\end{document}8% available ACLs and an acceleration of 4. ACLs. Second row: 4-fold Cartesian k-space trajectory with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$4\%$$\end{document}4% ACLs and an acceleration of 4. The inset shows a zoom on the image with corresponding absolute error. Our methods: fully learned filters (FLF) and fully learned filters and time conditioning (FLF+TC)

Interestingly, the shearlet model and the model with fully learned filters and time conditioning achieve performance comparable to TV. In the Cartesian case with only \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$4\%$$\end{document}4% of available ACLs, however, the latter shows a significant drop. We believe this, and the overall performance drop for the fat-suppressed data is due to the increased noise in the data, thus impairing quantitative comparison. This interpretation is supported by the qualitative results in Fig. 9, where the reference reconstructions appear very noisy. In contrast with TV, our models produce cleaner reconstructions with well-defined edges, as highlighted in the inset for the model with fully learned filters and time conditioning. Moreover, TV reconstructions occasionally retain undersampling artifacts, while the VN exhibits the expected strong artifacts when the number of ACLs is reduced.

Anatomical out of distribution

Tables 4 and 5 summarize quantitative results for the out-of-distribution experiments involving shifts in both the contrast mechanism and the underlying anatomy. For T1-weighted brain images, our models with fully learned convolutional filters clearly outperform all other methods, including the shearlet model. Specifically, our model with original time conditioning improves average PSNR by around 2 dB over TV across all k-space trajectories. Incorporating learned time conditioning provides an additional 0.1 dB gain.

Consistent with the previous out-of-distribution experiment, the end-to-end VN and DJS perform worse than our methods for all k-space trajectories. Notably, DJS exhibits extreme failure in most cases, yielding negative average PSNR values and extremely high standard deviations for all but the Gaussian k-space trajectory. Again, we investigate this behavior further in Appendix D.

The quantitative improvements in our method are mirrored in the qualitative reconstructions shown in Fig. 10. All reconstructions obtained with TV and a Cartesian k-space trajectory exhibit backfolding artifacts which are clearly visible in the insets of the first and second rows of Fig. 10. In contrast, our methods resolve these artifacts even under strong distribution shifts. As expected, small structures such as blood vessels (e.g., in the inset of the radial k-space trajectory in Fig. 10) are not well recovered by TV. Furthermore, both models with fully learned convolutional filters outperform the shearlet model and produce reconstructions with more pronounced vessels and reduced noise. Quantitative PI reconstruction results on the anatomical out-of-distribution dataset with T2 contrast. The rows alternate between PSNR, SSIM, and NMSE. The NMSE is scaled by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$10^2$$\end{document}102. All metrics are shown as mean ± unit standard deviation. Bold typeface indicates the best method. Our methods: shearlet (SH), fully learned filters (FLF), and fully learned filters and time conditioning (FLF+TC). Comparison methods: zero filled (ZF), total variation (TV) [6], end-to-end variational network (VN) [77], and Deep-JSense (DJS) [25]

a Rotated phase-encoding direction

T: k-space trajectory, C: Cartesian, R: radial, G: 2D Gaussian, A: acceleration, ACL: auto calibration lines

The results for T2-weighted brain images show a similar trend, with our top-performing model surpassing TV by 1 dB on average. Interestingly, the shearlet model drops below TV in terms of PSNR for this contrast, which we attribute to a higher noise level in the data. Both models with fully learned convolutional filters remain robust across all k-space trajectories, where the learned time conditioning provides an additional 0.2 dB improvement on average. The corresponding qualitative reconstructions are shown in Fig. 11. Here we highlight the Gaussian k-space trajectory in the last row, where we clearly observe typical staircasing artifacts in the TV reconstruction. Furthermore, neither TV nor the shearlet model recover the vertical edge separating the two brain hemispheres shown in the inset of the last row. By comparison, the models with fully learned filters (and time conditioning) preserve this structural detail with higher clarity.Fig. 10Qualitative PI comparison on the brain T1 dataset against TV. Uncertainty quantification

In this section, we qualitatively evaluate the uncertainty quantification of our framework. The proposed method (Algorithm 1) implements a stochastic map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{y} \mapsto \tilde{p}_{\textbf{X}, \mathbf {\Sigma } \mid \textbf{Y}=\textbf{y}}$$\end{document}y↦p~X,Σ∣Y=y that transforms undersampled measurements into a conditional distribution of reconstructions and coil sensitivities. From samples of this distribution, we compute pixel-wise variance maps to represent local uncertainty in the reconstructed images. We want to highlight that due to algorithmic heuristics and the intractability of the true likelihood, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{p}$$\end{document}p~ does not represent the true posterior distribution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{\textbf{X}, \mathbf {\Sigma } \mid \textbf{Y}=\textbf{y}}$$\end{document}pX,Σ∣Y=y and is an approximation. Nonetheless, these variance maps remain a useful tool for analyzing the spatial distribution of uncertainty across different image regions.Fig. 12Qualitative comparison of pixel-wise variance maps for the shearlet model, model with fully learned filters and the model with fully learned filters and time conditioning for a Cartesian k-space trajectory with 8% available ACLs and increasing acceleration factors between three and five. The left column shows the RSS reference and the zero-filled reconstruction. In the right columns, the rows alternate between the Monte Carlo estimates of the approximate posterior expectation reconstructions for the different methods and their corresponding pixel-wise variance maps. Our methods: shearlet (SH), fully learned filters (FLF), and fully learned filters and time conditioning (FLF+TC)

Qualitative comparison of pixel-wise variance maps for the shearlet model, model with fully learned filters and the model with fully learned filters and time conditioning for a Cartesian k-space trajectory with 8% available ACLs and increasing acceleration factors between three and five. The left column shows the RSS reference and the zero-filled reconstruction. In the right columns, the rows alternate between the Monte Carlo estimates of the approximate posterior expectation reconstructions for the different methods and their corresponding pixel-wise variance maps. Our methods: shearlet (SH), fully learned filters (FLF), and fully learned filters and time conditioning (FLF+TC)

We demonstrate this in the prototypical setting of a Cartesian k-space trajectory with 8 % available ACLs and increasing acceleration factors between three and five in Fig. 12. Specifically, we compute pixel-wise variance maps from 100 reconstructions for the shearlet model and the two models with fully learned convolutional filters. As expected, uncertainty increases alongside the acceleration factor due to the reduction in available data. This is particularly evident in regions with fine structural details, such as blood vessels in fat tissue, which become increasingly difficult to reconstruct. Furthermore, the shearlet model exhibits slightly higher variance in homogeneous regions, such as the femur and tibia, relative to the other two PoGMDM parameterizations. Finally, the model with learned time conditioning yields the highest pixel-wise variance across all acceleration factors. This qualitative observation is consistent with the quantitative results in Table 2, where the model with learned time conditioning shows slightly larger standard deviations across all comparison metrics.Fig. 13RSS null-space residuals for Cartesian k-space trajectories. In each block, the measured data have 8% available ACLs (left) and 4% available ACLs (right)

RSS null-space residuals for Cartesian k-space trajectories. In each block, the measured data have 8% available ACLs (left) and 4% available ACLs (right)

Coil sensitivities

In this section, we shift the focus to the estimated coil sensitivities of the joint reconstruction. Here, we compare the estimated coil sensitivities qualitatively to ESPIRiT [14] by investigating their RSS null-space residuals. The null-space residual of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i$$\end{document}i-th coil is given by27\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{\textbf{s}_i}{|\textbf{s}|^2_{\text {RSS}}} \left( \sum _{j=1}^c \bar{\textbf{s}}_j\textbf{x}_j \right) - \textbf{x}_i, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}si|s|RSS2∑j=1cs¯jxj-xi,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\cdot |_{\text {RSS}} $$\end{document}|·|RSS implements the map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{s} \mapsto \sqrt{\sum _{i=1}^c |\textbf{s}_i|^2} $$\end{document}s↦∑i=1c|si|2. Ideally, the residuals should only contain noise when the estimated coil sensitivities are exact. Similar to [6, 35], we compare the RSS reduction of the individual coil residuals, as shown in Fig. 13. While [14] provides slightly better coil sensitivity estimates when a larger number of ACLs are available, its performance degrades as the calibration region decreases. By contrast, our method delivers robust coil sensitivity estimates with less available data in the k-space center.

Ablation study

We now turn our focus on the influence of different parameter choices of the shearlet model and show numerical results for more efficient parameterization. Due to computational constraints, quantitative results in this section are generated by approximating the posterior expectation with a single reconstruction. Table 6 presents results for different number of scalings \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ j $$\end{document}j of the shearlet system. We observe a substantial improvement when increasing the scaling from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ j = 1 $$\end{document}j=1 to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ j = 2 $$\end{document}j=2. This is expected, as it allows the model to capture a broader range of directional components, thus improving its ability to represent fine structural details such as edges. Increasing to three scales yields minor improvements, while four scales slightly degrade reconstruction quality. We note that further hyperparameter tuning at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ j= 4 $$\end{document}j=4 might mitigate this. The results support the choice of a scaling of two, as it balances downstream performance and inference speed which grows with higher scaling.Table 6Quantitative PI reconstruction results for different shearlet scalings \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ j $$\end{document}j on the CORPD dataset. Quantitative PI reconstruction results for different shearlet scalings \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ j $$\end{document}j on the CORPD dataset. The rows alternate between PSNR, SSIM, and NMSE. The NMSE is scaled by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$10^2$$\end{document}102. All metrics are shown as mean ± unit standard deviation. Bold typeface indicates the best method. Comparison method: zero filled (ZF)

a Rotated phase-encoding direction

T: k-space trajectory, C: Cartesian, R: radial, G: 2D Gaussian, A: acceleration, ACL: auto calibration lines

In Sect. 5.1, we observed similarities in the learned potentials of the shearlet model (see the potentials in Fig. 1). To exploit these, we consider three sharing strategies: (i) sharing potentials within the vertical and horizontal cone at a given shearing scale \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ j $$\end{document}j (ii) sharing potentials across the vertical and horizontal cone at the same scale \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j$$\end{document}j and (iii) combining both (i) and (ii). For instance, in strategy (i) we share the first and fifth potential in the first row in Fig. 1, in (ii) we share the first and second row, and in (iii) we share both within and across the row reducing the number of different potentials per scale to three. This reduces the total number of parameters from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ 1558 $$\end{document}1558 to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ 676 $$\end{document}676. The quantitative results in Table 7 reveal that for all sharing strategies, the performance in terms of PSNR stays within \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \pm 0.12$$\end{document}±0.12 dB of the shearlet baseline without sharing. Interestingly, the configuration with the lowest parameter count slightly outperforms the baseline on the Gaussian k-space trajectory, which may suggest that the grid search on the limited validation set did not identify optimal hyperparameters here.
